ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e123	Ambulance aeroplanes are an important part of health preparedness in Norway. Especially in North Norway, this service is essential for people to feel safe about getting needed health care. I therefore look very seriously at the situation that has arisen. Night until Saturday 28 April, all the ambulance aeroplanes were on the ground, and in time, for periods of time, there has been a reduction in the availability of ambulances, especially in Finnmark. This is a difficult period. At the same time, health services show their best interest. Both personnel and extra resources are mobilised to find solutions that best safeguard the security of the population. This applies to all actors. Although the situation has been challenging for healthcare and those who work there and that some patients have had to wait, this has not yet had serious consequences for life and health. I would like to thank everyone who is on the verge of such extraordinary situations. The overall responsibility for all air ambulances in Norway lies with the Health Agency. The entity is owned by the four regional health establishments. The situation that has now arisen has been specifically followed up by Health Nord on their behalf. It is also this region that has had the greatest preparedness challenges. The acquisition process itself was in its time led by Health Mid-Norway, who had the chief executive at that time. As the owner of the regional health companies and the top manager, I have had close dialogue with Health Nord, and through them also with the Air ambulance service. I have been to Bodø and Tromsø and have been briefed on how those responsible for the provision of services are planning to deal with the preparedness ahead, and I have met those who are in the middle of the situation. A reporting system is in place to report on the emergency situation at the aerodromes in the health regions on a daily basis, including: to the Department, the Directorate of Health and the State. The Ministry of Health and Health asked the Directorate of Health on Monday 7 May to contact the Defence for helicopter assistance. The Health Directorate then asked the Defence Operational Headquarters to do so in an ordinary manner and in line with an established agreement. The defense put in two manned helicopters in Finnmark last week. Health Nord, as I have asked, has developed a plan of action, both in the short and long term. It contains several compensatory measures that may be applied in the event of the loss of ambulance aeroplanes. The plan must necessarily be further developed and updated as the situation develops, not least in the event of changes in the capacity of Air Transport AS to provide services. The main elements of the plan are: In addition to the two mentioned Defense Helicopters, one additional flight from Babcock is leased. Babcock will also be able to offer a jet plane for service in Norway from the next week, if necessary. Action is also being taken to strengthen staffing in the AMK Central and Air Coordination Centres in Tromsø and to strengthen emergency medical preparedness locally. There are mobilised resources that strengthen the other ambulance service, and alternative ambulance resources are requested to be used when appropriate and possible. It is assessed on an ongoing basis how to dispose of the total air ambulance resources available. This is in line with practices that are established when during periods there is a reduced preparedness in parts of the emergency medical chain, e.g. when bad weather makes it difficult to fly. Air transport has taken action to improve the availability of pilots in the future. Ten-twelve new pilots will be ready for duty in June/July. As from 1 June of this year, a new contract shall be opened for ambulance helicopters. It allows for the insertion of two additional reserve helicopters into operation in addition to the normal standby. In their longer-term plan, the Air ambulance service has also looked at the possibilities of hiring several aircraft from external suppliers, including the possibility for Babcock to take over responsibility for some bases before 1 July 2019 – this if air transport is unable to deliver sufficient services during their contractual period. The latest situation report suggests that preparedness has also been reduced today, but that it improves over a week. Today, four out of nine aircraft are on standby all day, while two are on standby parts of the day. Tomorrow six out of nine aircraft are expected to be on standby all day, while two are on standby much of the day. After this, there are currently no known withdrawals in the coming weeks. The emergency situation must also be seen in the context of the provision of additional resources, as mentioned above. With Norway’s geography and housing patterns, the Air Ambulance Service is central to effective treatment, transport and preparedness in the event of acute illness or injury. The service shall contribute to equivalent healthcare provision and is a prerequisite for patients to benefit from modern treatment provision in time-critical conditions when hospitalisation is far away. In North Norway, especially in Finnmark, ambulances are an important response resource that performs a large number of immediate assistance tasks, often with a high degree of urgency. Ambulance aeroplanes have the advantage that they are less vulnerable to severe weather conditions than the helicopters are. Ambulance flights also have an important role to play in ensuring transport between hospitals when needed. 35 pst. of the missions of the ambulance aeroplanes, are intended to be reinstatements of patients who have received specialised treatment. It is this type of mission that is deprioritised when planes are on the ground and other missions are urgent. The air ambulance service currently has 13 ambulance helicopters, which are operated from 12 bases, and 9 ambulance aircraft, which are operated from 7 bases. It is also an agreement with the Ministry of Justice and Security that rescue helicopters may be used for air ambulance operations. All vessels are equipped with advanced medical monitoring and treatment equipment. It allows medical intervention on site and intensive treatment during transportation. About 20,000 patients are assisted each year by the Air ambulance Service. Nearly half are transported by ambulance aircraft. Of these, 35 are pst. so-called readmission operations. Air ambulance services are an important complement to the local emergency services, which are medical and car and boat ambulance services. In Norway, nearly 98 psts are resolved. of all sick transport services by car ambulances. The air ambulance service solves about 2 pst. of the missions, but accounts for a significantly higher share in North Norway and, in particular, in Finnmark. When the national air ambulance service was established in 1988, it was decided that the operational part of the service, i.e. the operation of the service helicopters and aircraft, should be purchased through a public tender with real competition. After the health enterprise reform in 2012, care was transferred to regional health enterprises. A joint subsidiary was established in 2004, now organised as a health business: the HF Air ambulance service. Air ambulance services enter into contracts with private companies for the flight and helicopter services. The aeroplane and helicopter contracts are concluded in accordance with regular tendering procedures. In the final bidding round, the contracts had a duration of six years, with an option for an extension of up to five years for aeroplanes and four years for helicopters. When tenders are made at regular intervals, this contributes to the renewal of the aircraft fleet and to an increase in quality. This is a good thing for the people. Since the establishment of the company in 2004, the Air ambulance Service has carried out a total of five acquisitions of air ambulance services, including the agreement currently under discussion. These have been contracts in which there are significant amounts, without the acquisitions leading to complaints to the Public Acquisitions Complaint Board and without any legal added value. No business transfer has been required in any of these acquisitions. Until 1994, there were several operators in the service. In 1994, Møreflug became the sole operator following a tendering procedure. Mortar aircraft were merged with Air Transport in 1995 and the name was then ‘Air Transport’. Air transport was also given operator responsibility during the contractual periods from 2001 to 2009. Competition has been somewhat limited. In 2009, Norwegian Air ambulance submitted tenders on some bases, but not the full service. The acquisition of new ambulance aircraft services started in the autumn of 2015, first by identifying the current service and finding out what the service needed. Together with Hospital Purchases – at the time HINAS – the Air Ambulance Service established a joint project management. A separate strategy paper was prepared for the acquisition where several alternative solutions were highlighted. The strategy paper was then sent to nearly 500 agencies and social interests in February 2016. There were 44 recordings and 27 hearing replies. On 14 November 2016, regional health companies adopted a revised strategy paper for the ambulance flight service, on which the current flight and base structure was used for the acquisition of ambulance aircraft services with effect from 1 July 2019. Tenders and tender criteria were drawn up in line with the adopted strategy plan. This work also involved many stakeholders: the Air ambulance Service and Hospital Purchases, representatives of the service’s nurses and doctors as well as the security services. Three evaluation rounds and two negotiating meetings were carried out. Quality was improved during the negotiations and the price was reduced. There were a total of 367 requirements on the basis of competition and the offers were ranked after 95 of them. The others were mandatory requirements and requirements to be met during the contract period. In view of the overall value of the contract, the four managing directors of the regional health establishments wished to obtain a specific mandate from their pre-contractual boards. A joint administrative case was developed in May 2017, in which each of the Boards was asked to give its Executive Director an authorisation within the scope outlined in the Board of Directors. In the board case, it was informed of the execution of the tender process and that there were two offers each competing against each other. Furthermore, information was provided on the quality requirements used. Estimates of the costs of the new contract were also provided. The award of the contract was decided and published in June 2017. Air transport, which lost its competitive position, complained about the award and warned that they considered their request for a temporary injection. Therefore, in line with the legislation and good business practice, the contract signature was postponed. However, air transport failed to request temporary supply and did not complain to the complainant about public acquisitions. There is no basis for claiming other than that competition has been implemented in line with the basic principles of procurement law: competition, non-discrimination, predictability, transparency and reprobability. After the acquisition process was completed, the air ambulance service established a project of reception with a dedicated project manager to follow up Babcock until launch. A cooperation body with representatives of the main stakeholders has also been established. Babcock is now working to establish a Norwegian company for the services to be provided through the agreement with the Air ambulance Service. Babcock will also carry out the maintenance of the ambulance aircraft in North Norway, and they are looking at the possibilities to do so in Tromsø, as today. In addition, they will have some decentralised maintenance on the bases. The owner group is currently the operator of the air ambulance service in a number of countries, including Sweden and Finland. Babcock has presented a detailed progress plan and has a full-time project manager to carry out the preparations. The current contract also requires the current operator, Air Transport, to contribute to a seamless transition to a new contract. It is important that air transport is aware of this responsibility. That is why I had a meeting yesterday with the Chairman and CEO of Air Transport. In this meeting I got clear assurances that they will do what they can to comply with this part of the contract. There shall be a meeting later today between the air transport and air ambulance management, where this is also a topic. The contract with Babcock lasts from 1 July 2019 to 2025, with a possible extension to 2030. The contract helps to increase the quality of the service. With effect from 1 July 2019, it shall take effect, inter alia: new runway aeroplanes on all bases. These aeroplanes will have better navigation equipment and better safety equipment on board and will fly faster than today. The new aeroplanes will have a medical facility that allows for more specialised treatment on board. The service also gets a medium-sized jet plane on the Gardermoen, which replaces one of today's propeller planes. This can reach Svalbard and North Norway with a lot of equipment and more doctors and nurses on board. A herd shall be introduced into the rear guard, which may be collected in the event of sickness and absence. This is new and provides the service with increased availability and better preparedness compared to today’s contract. The award criteria ‘quality’ and ‘price’ were weighted in the evaluation of the offers by 60 pst respectively. and 40 pst. All offers scored high on quality. On the scale used, one of the air transport offers was best scored with a score of 10, while the selected offer from Babcock scored 9.51. The decisive thing was that air transport was significantly higher in price: 47 million. kr annually, or close to 300 mill. kr during the first six-year period. From 2019, the Ambulance Aviation Service will cost approx. One hundred million. kr more than it does today. This means that the expenditure of regional health enterprises is rising from about 400 million. kr in the year to approx. 500 million. kr annually. On 8 September 2016, my State Secretary Anne Grethe Erlandsen held a meeting with the Norwegian Flyer Federation. This was the theme of the air ambulance service tendering process. The Führerverbund expressed its concerns during the meeting, and they were particularly concerned about not having to face a situation where a foreign operator was brought in. They considered it essential that Norwegian labour law should apply. With a foreign company, they meant, for example: that a strike could not be stopped by forced pay. This, of course, was an important point. The air ambulance service required the agreements to be governed by Norwegian law, including mediation and arbitration proceedings before Norwegian courts. The contract with Babcock shows that they establish a Norwegian company to manage the Norwegian business. At the meeting with the State Secretary, it was agreed that the Norwegian Führer Association would draw up a note of its concerns and that the Department would pass it on to the Air ambulance Service. The Ministry forwarded the note to the head of the air ambulance service as agreed on 14 September 2016. Since the result of the tendering process became known last year, there have been some plays related to the terms of the new contract with a new operator. In connection with Babcock’s announcement of pilot positions at the end of last year, the Führerverbund concerned, inter alia: the recruitment situation – this is based on the expectation that they would have worse working conditions than in air transport. In the last few months, today’s supplier has had problems delivering the necessary preparedness in the service to which the company has committed itself. In parts of March, a large proportion of air transport pilots were out of regular service. This must be seen in the context of redundancies and that the company employs experienced pilots to train new staff to enter and replace those who end up. As a result, it is difficult to cover all the guards. The situation that arose at the end of April, when the ambulance planes all over the country were on the ground, came after that there was no solution in the negotiations between the Norwegian Flyer Federation and Babcock about the transfer of the pilots to the new company. The Führerbunder said in meeting me that they felt that Babcock had gone back on previous offers. I am pleased that Babcock and NHO have now come up with a new offer, and that there is dialogue between the parties. In the NOU 2015, the Emergency Committee considered that the issue of public operation of the air ambulance service should be addressed. The Norwegian Air Ambulance Foundation expressed in the hearing that such an investigation would have to cover more than just public operations. Aviation is a highly complex sector, both technically and safely. Aviation requires a completely different competence from that currently held by healthcare. That is why, in my opinion, it is not a competence that it is natural for the specialist health service to build up on its own. The air ambulance service in Norway was established, as mentioned earlier, as a national service in 1988. It has always been delivered by private suppliers. The private actors have been pioneers in the development and operation of the service, and we have a long and good tradition of cooperation. This case shows that operator switching can be challenging. Good and binding agreements and systematic preparations are important for success. A similar acquisition for a new contract has recently been carried out for the ambulance helicopter service. The contract was concluded in June 2016 and implies that Norsk Luftambulanse AS is the operator of all its ambulance helicopters from 1 June of this year. Air transport AS, which has been the operator on three of the bases, thus leaves as an operator. There have been no particular problems with the transition from Air Transport to Norwegian Air Ambulance, which is owned by the Norwegian Air Ambulance Foundation. A central issue in this case is the issue of business transfer, i.e. the requirement for staff to be transferred from air transport to Babcock at the time of operator change. As you know, it has not been made a requirement in this tender that a transfer of business should be made. It is also not common for the specialist health service to lay down other conditions for the transfer of activities in tendering procedures for the purchase of healthcare. The air ambulance service expressed in connection with the acquisition that the transfers to a new operator, if any, could lead to a transfer of activities in line with the provisions of the Working Environment Act. In this case, it was assessed that it was not possible to conclude on this before the conclusion of the contract. This was something that any new service provider would have to consider in their offer. The air ambulance service informed the operators about these conditions. The terms and conditions of aviation have changed in recent years. In general, wage growth has been reduced and pension systems changed. I do not know and should not know the details of the salary offers from the new operator. However, the air ambulance service has been aware that the pay conditions for the pilots of Babcock are competitive with the major commercial carriers in Norway. The term ‘social dumping’, which some have used, is therefore not relevant in this context. At the same time, of course, I have a lot of understanding that no one wants to go down in pay. Yesterday Marianne Telle chose to resign as director of Health North. She did this for the sake of Health North and the rest of the board's ability to lead the health region in this demanding period. It's a wish that I respect, and I'm grateful for the job she's done. I would like to stress that no information has been provided suggesting that Marianne Telle has participated in the procurement process in such a way that it has influenced the process, or has received information that has given her benefits in her management position. Yesterday, a representative proposal was made to cancel the tender with Babcock and to continue the agreement between the Air ambulance service and air transport. There is nothing about the situation that has now arisen to suggest that Babcock is unable to provide air ambulance services in line with the contract. Until 1 July 2019, the current operator, Air Transport, is responsible for the service and contributing to a seamless transition to a new supplier. The contract with Babcock has been concluded legally and cannot be reversed without significant costs and legal consequences for regional health businesses. Of course, any contract can be concluded, but in this context it will have major economic consequences for healthcare, whether through legal conciliation or legal proceedings. Babcock has several aeroplanes and a production simulator. To date, they have used considerable resources to discharge the contract. The costs incurred are not only those for which one becomes liable but also those for which future income is due. These are significant amounts, which would then have to go at the expense of patient treatment. An extension of today’s air transport contract without a new bidding round would initially be illegal. By concluding the agreement with Babcock and continuing to cooperate with today's operator, we would therefore have stood without a legal service offer to the country's population. We would also not have received the technical and emergency improvement of the service from the next summer, which I have described earlier. I'll strongly warn you that we're going into such a situation. It is still more than one year for the new operator to assume responsibility for the service. It is very unfortunate if the population over a long period of time should feel insecure about the ability of health services to maintain their preparedness and provide the necessary health care in the event of acute illness or injury. All parties have served the service as normal. Plans are now being made to deal with possible response interruptions until the acquisition on 1 July next year. This case has also raised a political discussion regarding the use of private operators and tenders. I would like to point out that the Air ambulance service has had good and constructive cooperation with private operators over several decades with changing governments and magnification. We should not forget that it was private who took the initiative for medical personnel ambulance helicopters and subsequently established the Norwegian Air Ambulance Foundation. Very many Nordics have supported the foundation through both membership and financial contributions. After 1988, the air ambulance was a publicly funded national service, but still provided by private individuals. The Foundation’s work continues to be largely publicised, which also contributes significantly to research and innovation in emergency medicine. If the operational responsibility for which Norsk Luftambulance is responsible today should have been transferred to the public, I am sure that it would also have been controversial and led to significant political discussion. In acquisition processes, it is necessarily the case that some will not compete. We have to respect that. The Code of Public Acquisition is a tool to ensure the efficient and fair use of public resources. It also contributes to a better health service for the population. I am pleased that there now seems to be contact between the parties to find a solution. It is essential that all actors do their utmost to secure the service in the future. I feel that the new operator is now clear that they want to take over all the pilots with their skills. That's a good starting point.

I therefore expect all parties to settle down, so that we can return to a normal emergency situation as soon as possible – this in order to ensure that patients and residents in North Norway will again be fully confident that they will get help when they need it, regardless of where in North Norway they live.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e320	The crisis in the north would never happen. There should have been safe public operation or long-term cooperation with ideals. When you first choose to commercialise the offer further, you would never have liked these requirements, or with a lack of business transfer requirements. In the long north, the distances are so huge that life will be lost without an ambulance plane. From Honningsvåg to Hammerfest, it takes almost three hours by car. It's their local hospital. The decentralised food supply requires the ability to carry incubators by plane. From Vardø to the hospital in Tromsø – for very serious, acute cases – it takes 11.5 hours by car. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e521	The Biotechnology Act regulates very many important aspects of biomedicine, research and development with an enormous impact on many people. Good legislation in this field should balance important ethical considerations and the right of the population to good, safe healthcare. It may seem like a dilemma and a demanding balance, but historically, these challenges have proved to be something the world has solved in a good way; that's my claim. We are living today with safe medical advances would have been ahead of us. Norway, like other parts of the world, has made strict demands and has taken value considerations into account in what is possible and what should be allowed. Fortunately, the scare images we know from previous years, about the cultivation of genetically perfect humans, cloning or sorting, have not come true. The world has proved to be more responsible than the fear of the opposers, and inequality in humans has a self-esteem that not only we in the Nordic countries or other Western countries will hold back. The development of biomedicine has turned in the direction that we want to help people to live as good a life as possible based on their natural assumptions. For people with hereditary, serious or fatal diseases, it has meant new hope, new opportunities for treatment and relief, or a chance to produce their own children without fear of further serious diseases. The Labour Party believes this is a positive development that is not about stopping progress, but ensuring that the ethically strict requirements we set for research and development are maintained and respected for the benefit of people. When the Biotechnology Act was adopted in 2003, Stortinget decided that the law should be evaluated after five years of practice. The government we have today delivered the message at the end of the spring session in June 2017. It's probably an expression that in these questions there's been a standstill because of a slightly different majority of the Biggest. The communication highlights the different themes of the law, which the case-rapporteur explains well, but the government has not ventured to take a decision, leaving it to the majority of people to conclude. The attitude we are discussing today reflects well the majority of the Stortinge in matters that have been discussed for many years. For the Labour Party, this means that today we are involved in the formation of a majority in order to lift the ban on egg donation. We believe that egg donation of unfertilized eggs is basically about equality between women and men, because in couples where the man, for example. is infertile, has since about 1930 been able to receive help in the form of sperm donation. For many years now, we have had safe, good methods of harvesting eggs from women, and there are no longer rational reasons why women should not be able to help others voluntarily by donating. The fact that women can be exploited in vulnerable situations by pressure for egg donation would be very effective through strict restrictions on possible financial compensation, a question that is important for the Stortinge to come back to when the law comes to retreatment. That, moreover, women should be less able to choose for themselves whether to donate eggs away than men who have been able to donate semen, bottoms in attitudes that the Workers' Party does not stand behind. The working party wants egg donation to take place under the same conditions as sperm donation, with the child’s right to know its origin, which means that any donor child has the right to know its origin at the age of 18. We see no need to change the exact limit of sperm donation. The Labour Party will maintain the ban on surrogati in Norway and support the government in the fact that the ban on surrogati is a sufficient indication of Norway’s position on this and that the use of surrogati should not be punishable. This is for the sake of the eventually quite many surrogate children growing up in Norway. It is difficult to imagine how parents can be punished without this having negative consequences for their children. That is the basis of the Workers' Party's position. Other representatives from my party will speak in the debate on reproductive health. But for us, in particular, the question of foetal diagnostics and foetal medicine – and I'll talk a little more about it – will follow the good tradition of the country's competent foetal medical profession, namely the ability to save lives and start effective treatment as early as possible, where there are known and effective ways to help. For the Workers' Party, the opportunity to help and treat has led our approach to the Biotechnology Act. Preventing pain, suffering and early death for hereditary disease by using new medical methods has been important for the Workers' Party's attitude to this law. We believe that, in the past, we have managed to balance important ethical considerations against each other and yet are clear to get through changes that are right, even though the debate towards that conclusion has often been difficult. Many of us still remember the Mehmet case – the bleeding boy who was dependent on healthy stem cells. Finally, in the Workers' Party, we allowed parents to try to make siblings – a healthy child – to Mehmet via the PGD method, preimplantation diagnostics. These were not easy debates. If we were to make the fresh sibling as a medium just for others, the opponents said at that time. But parents aren't like that. Everyone who has children knows that you're going to love the new child just as much, and that the new child will live his whole life in the certainty that it saved his big brother's life. The danger of having a new sick child also weighed heavily to allow parents to use this method in their time. Only this one difficult case shows that there are many ethical issues that arise in the development of biotechnology, and the question of biotechnology is rarely black and white. In this setting, Stortinget is still moving a few spots to give better help to families who are involuntary orphans, but also to families who have serious hereditary diseases. The Workers' Party is pleased to contribute. Then I'll bring up proposal No. 1, from the Workers' Party, SV and Left.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e612	Biotechnology creates both new opportunities and ethical dilemmas. In the right, we assume that ethics and values should set the framework for the use of technology, not vice versa. At the same time, ethical considerations and values need to be discussed and weighed up in the light of societal developments and the opportunities offered by technology. All people have an inherent and inviolable self-value. This is the basis of all the Right’s position in this matter. As a result, all humans have the same value, regardless of their characteristics, abilities and level of function. Human life cannot therefore be graduated or ranked. It is also due to the fact that no human being is to be used as a medium for other humans. We want a society where there's room for everyone. At the same time, the Right places emphasis on the individual's freedom, personal responsibility and the right to determine his or her own life. This is particularly true in matters relating to private matters. The state must give room for different choices in life and not unduly restrict the individual’s or family’s right to shape their own future. Although the individual should have great freedom in private matters, it is legitimate to regulate matters which also affect the rights of other people and important social interests. Biotechnology and genetic engineering include: the protection of the rights of children and the consideration of the living conditions of future generations could imply restrictions on the individual's self-determination. Another aspect that needs to be taken into account in this debate is the administrative responsibility that we as politicians have. This implies that today's generation must seek to give future generations at least as good living conditions as today. The policy must therefore ensure that the medical and technological advances that we allow will benefit people. Pre-precautionary considerations must therefore guide issues where medical treatment may have adverse and irreversible consequences for our descendants. Right will develop a policy for biotechnology and genetic engineering based on an assessment of relevant ethical considerations and values, in the light of medical and technological opportunities, international conventions, national and international social conditions. Regulations of other countries may also be a relevant consideration, but one must always consider them in the light of our own views on ethics, values and what is an appropriate national regulation. The fact that something is allowed in another country should not only be a decisive argument for something that otherwise violates what is considered nationally to be fundamental ethical considerations. The evaluation we are examining today also addresses questions related to gene therapy. Gene therapy involves making changes to our genetic material to treat disease or affect biological functions. The right is in favour of gene therapy and re-editing providing better medical treatment for a variety of diseases and conditions. The use of somatic gene therapy, which involves changes in the patient’s own cells without being inherited, must be subject to a regular assessment of the medical benefits, costs and risks to the individual. This implies that a serious disease need not necessarily exist in order to apply this technology. On the other hand, we will not allow gene therapy and re-editing which do not have a medical purpose, but which only aims to improve human abilities and properties. This will break with the value of man's inviolable self-esteem and undermine the goal of a society with space for all. Genotherapy in germ cells, embryos or fertilised eggs involving permanent changes in heritable facilities involves a significant ethical dilemma due to the potential risk of incalculable and unfortunate consequences for future generations, despite the fact that such treatment could potentially provide better health and living conditions for humans. Therefore, in cases where gene therapy and re-editation lead to changes in heritable facilities, the Right will place crucial emphasis on pre-war considerations. This implies that such treatment should be allowed only if the risk of undesirable effects can be excluded, including for future generations. At the same time, more research on gene therapy should be opened up in order to provide us with more knowledge and a better basis for decision-making. A fertilized egg is the seed of a human life. This suggests a need for a framework and regulation of how the fertilised egg is treated. Right-wing believes that fertilisation of eggs for research purposes alone will mean an instrumental approach to human life that cannot be accepted. Under some conditions, research into fertilised eggs that are prolific and in any case to be destroyed may be legitimate. The right considers that this can only be allowed for up to 14 days after conception, as a new development of an embryo starts from this stage. Within this framework, genetic modification of surplus fertilised eggs to be destroyed may also be permitted by a Right-View if this may provide knowledge essential to contributing to better health and living conditions. Right-wing believes that embryo research will cross a fundamental ethical boundary that is important for the protection of human inviolable self-value. Such research will make one life a means of another, and we therefore oppose allowing such research. Many technological advances make it possible for the involuntary childless to have children. Right-wing believes that individuals should have great freedom to mold their own family life and to make choices for themselves and their own. However, the regulation in this area must also be based on the rights of the child and the need for protection. Individuals should have the freedom and responsibility to make good choices in life for themselves and their families. The family is an important foundation of society, and there must be room for different forms of life and family life. At its national meeting in spring, the right decided that the offer of assisted fertilisation must be extended to the same extent if the conditions for providing the child with safe and good growth conditions are met in the individual case. This requirement must apply mutatis mutandis to couples offered assisted fertilisation. This view is also evident from our comments in the position of the case. At the same time, each human being has the right to know his biological origin. This can be of fundamental importance in understanding and knowing its identity, its background and its history. All donor children born by donation in Norway should therefore have the right to know their biological origin at the age of 15 years. Therefore, the right considers that only semen donation with a known donor should be allowed. Fortunately, lowering the age limit from 18 to 15 will have a majority in committee. Egg donation can give women and couples with fertility problems an opportunity to have their own biological children. Egg donation means that a child has both an egg standard and a mother who has brought the child forward. Allowing egg donation will contribute to equal treatment of couples where the man is infertile, and couples where the woman is infertile. Technological developments have led to low-risk intervention and treatment associated with egg donation. This is also a choice the woman takes herself. In the case of egg donation, which in the case of normal pregnancy, a biological relationship is established between mother and child through pregnancy. If the donor egg is fertilised with the partner’s sperm, the child will have a genetic relationship with the father and biological relationship with the mother. In the case of semen donation, the child is only genetically related to the mother. Egg donation is therefore not directly comparable to semen donation. However, the right considers that the differences are not large enough to justify that one should be prohibited, while the other is allowed. Therefore, the right will allow egg donation in Norway, which is agreed by a majority of the Health Committee. Egg donation must be allowed, as in the case of semen donation, only with a known donor and parents of children born after egg or semen donation, obligations to tell the child about it as soon as it is advisable. The conditions for egg donation must be regulated. Important questions to clarify are the age of the donor and the recipient, the combination of egg and semen donation and economic conditions around donation and treatment. Today, the storage of unfertilised eggs and ovaries is reserved for women meeting the requirements for assisted fertilisation or for women undergoing treatment that may impair fertility. In the Biotechnology Communication, the Government proposes that the right to the storage of unfertilised eggs and ovary tissues on a medical basis should also include medical conditions that may lead to infertility at an early age. This is supported today by a unanimous committee. On the other hand, there is a question of egg and ovary storage should be allowed for all women, including on a non-medical basis. It is widely known that egg quality decreases with the age of the woman and that women have a shorter reproductive period than men. From the age of 32, female fertility is gradually reduced. This process accelerates from the age of 37 and women cannot be expected to be fertile after age 45. The right considers that the storage limit for frozen ova and embryos after assisted fertilisation should be considered, but that an offer of storage should not be allowed for all women, as it could lead to a materialisation of reproduction and contribute to the development of pressure on the woman from e.g.. employers or others to postpone pregnancy to a more ‘appropriate’ time. With this, pregnancy will be seen more as something that can be adapted, deferred and down-prioritized. In addition to being negative in itself, this will contribute to a further increase in the mean age of pregnant women. In general, it is assumed that the risk of complications during pregnancy and childbirth increases with the age of the woman. It is believed that it is also better for the child with parents who are not much above fertile age. The evaluation also addresses the question of surrogati. Surrogati can give childless persons an opportunity to have children. However, the right considers that surrogati breaks the natural bond between the child and the mother who gives birth to it, in a way that can impose a significant burden on both the child and surrogacy mother. This poses a risk both to the child and to the surrogate mother that cannot be balanced for the sake of adults who want children. In addition, commercial surrogati involves an unacceptable instrumentalisation of the pregnancy that makes both the body of the surrogacy mother and the child a potential commodity. This can be particularly problematic when surrogacy has few resources, which limits her real freedom to make a choice that involves major ethical dilemmas. Another question relates to foetal diagnostics. Foetal diagnostics can provide important information on the health or genetic properties of the foetus. This must be distinguished from studies in the context of maternity care, for example. routine ultrasound examination. The right considers that there should still be a provision for routine examinations as part of maternity care, where the intention is to follow the development of the child. On the other hand, the public shall not facilitate routine screening of the characteristics, conditions or disease of a foetus without a specific risk to this effect. The right considers that the public offer of foetal diagnostics must be primarily for medical purposes with a view to diagnosing serious conditions in the child who can be prevented or treated or who requires a specific follow-up by means of maternity care. The current legislation on the provision of foetal diagnostics only exceptionally, taking into account the life situation of pregnant women, should be continued. An extension of the offer of study and diagnostics without medical justification could lead to a pressure to sort away fetuses based on characteristics, which would undermine the value of a society with space for all. In addition, such offers would imply that the scarce resources of healthcare are used for offers that are not necessary on the basis of a medical justification. There will not be enough professional competence or capacity in healthcare to provide all a routine offer of an examination of all foetuses with a view to detecting possible diseases or conditions. When foetal diagnostics are offered, the most gentle and effective diagnostic method should be offered to pregnant women. Pregnancies and couples who choose to accept offers of foetal diagnostics shall be offered genetic guidance. The prohibition on disclosing sex before the 12th week of pregnancy, unless there is a risk of serious sexually transmitted disease, is maintained. This prevents the child’s gender from becoming a relevant consideration in the assessment of abortion. The Committee's position makes it clear what the Committee believes and what the parties think about the various questions we have been asked to discuss. I look forward to a further debate on the matter, which will have its final outcome when the bill is presented. That's when we change the legal position of these questions. Today we come with our opinions to the government for the further work on the billing proposition.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e663	It's going to be line change.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e808	So today we are going to deal with the biotechnology notification, an evaluation of the biotechnology law. And is it so important that there will be a public offer for single women to have children? Without having partners and without having toil with disease and infertility, it is difficult to understand that this should then be an offer for those with resources. The majority write in a note that there is ‘property’ to determine whether an assisted fertilisation is available if one is single, but it should be a bit of a judgment job the state takes on when the health care system defines and determines which of the single women who have well enough networks and resources to provide assisted fertilisation. The important thing here is that a break with the principle that assisted fertilisation should be an offer within certain frameworks for those who are struggling to have children, i.e. that it is a medical issue. Being single is no disease. Children have the right to two parents. The State should make no provision for anything else. The Government proposes that all persons who are to be offered assisted fertilisation should be evaluated both medically and from the point of view of the couple. Clearer and common guidelines for this evaluation of care shall be introduced. The centre party supports this. However, we do not support the introduction of child-care certification requirements for assisted fertilisation. According to the government, this is a police certificate indicating whether the person is sighted, accused, has taken counsel or has been convicted of crimes against several criminal orders, such as the police. sexual law offences, murders, drug law offences and gross violence and crime. A child care certificate is so extensive and not a certificate that should only be able to detect sexual offences. We thought that the border rise would be difficult: where does the border go? What types of previous crimes and convictions are not consistent with being a parent? Is that right? Is it drug law? The centre party suggests that no such requirement for a child care certificate is introduced. So to the proposal to let healthy women get the right to store egg cells completely regardless of whether there is any medical reason for it: Fresh women will be offered from the state to freeze eggs, such as in the case, so that one can have young and healthy eggs in reserve – fun to have when a later in life is considering having children! First, this is at most a driver to make children an order item. Secondly, there are many other things the welfare state should spend its resources on than providing healthy women with this. Talking about a big asset, as the Labour Party does, doesn't help. If it is so important for the Labour Party, the Progress Party and the Left to make this a public offer, then it is a paradox that only those who can afford it should be involved. However, it is obvious that a multiple will open for mitochondrial donation. That means that the child will receive genetic heritage from three parents. That it's a little genetic material, I thought it was quite obvious that a person argues with. A gene is a gene. And it's a professional discussion whether the method is medically safe. A layer of genetic changes in germ cells either before or after conception. I didn't think Norway would allow this. But no, the Labour Party, the Progress Party and the Left in the driver's seat ask the government to look to the United Kingdom to learn more and to introduce this in Norway when this is safe. But where did the principle come from? Is there no limit anymore, no frame around it to create a healthy fetus? When it comes to preimplantation diagnostics, the Centre Party is concerned not to liberalise, but we still support the proposal that PDG can be granted even if the applicant has not demonstrated carrier status, if one parent has 50 pst. probability of serious hereditary disease that cannot be treated. For us, this is about the right not to know if one will get sick in the future, and about respect for the parents who do not want to test. The center party is ready that we will not allow research on fertilized eggs. We want to keep up with today's requirements for foetal diagnostics. We'll go against providing the NIPT blood test for all pregnant women. We want the early ultrasound offer to be regulated as today and not become a routine offer to healthy women. We know that only has a medical purpose to diagnose if the fetus has trisomi and Down's syndrome. It does not lead to better health for mother and child, and it must be a central premise. So to genetic self-tests: the Biotechnology Council's plurality suggests that genetic self-tests with diagnostic or therapeutic purposes should only be allowed in the health care system. We also mean that, so we're doing this again in a proposal. Otherwise, we'll support the reduction in the age limit for getting a white donor's identity, from 18 to 15. We do not support a generic extended egg storage time, but we open it so that it can be applied for in some cases. With this, I will present the proposal of the Centre Party in the setting. Then I would also like to inform you that we are going to support proposal No. 20, the Labour Party's proposal which is pending in the Chamber today.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e978	First, I would like to thank the committee chairman and the case rapporteur for an unusually good and balanced presentation. I know this takes her personally, and therefore there is even greater reason to give her thanks for the article. Laws are often said to be stronger ethics. In this way, the Storting is today invited to draw a snapshot of an ethical development that goes faster than many of us like. Biotechnology offers great opportunities to cure and relieve, but it also asks us eternal questions about what it is to be human, and how far we can intervene in the ecology of the body and the soul, to use such a concept. Yesterday we discussed an adjustment of the inflation target from 2.5 to 2 p.m., but as I followed the debate, I realized that we would like to see a downward adjustment of the inflation target of biotechnology, so that we could take on more of the rapid changes that are taking place. It is a symptom of this rapid development that we have had to postpone this spring's deadline for the submission of the Committee's position in order to allow a few party meetings to address important ethical issues – before they soon become larger in legal texts. Now that we're going to review the law, some say it's high time. It has not been revised for 15 years. There are many controversial and difficult questions here, so it explains that neither the red-green nor the current government has been able to provide an evaluation for the Storting before now. The best thing that can happen to a child is to be brought into a family that longs for the child, who goes through fire and water to do its best. The greatest change in the moral reflection on biotechnological themes has probably been the upgrade of the child's social environment in recent years, while the importance of genetic origin has been reduced. The world is full of examples of betrayals against children who have two biological parents, parents who fail the task and expose their offspring to indescribable abuse. Children have no guarantee of a good upbringing, even if everything has happened according to the nature method – with one man and one woman present. As a result of the ethical and technological developments in the years to come, we are going to see an abundance of family constellations, relationships that we today find unfamiliar and strange, but which are going to hear the normal state in a few years' time. The advantage of having lived for a while is that I know how short a time since it is that a child with one caregiver was stigmatised and looked down on. We are currently in a situation where more and more people see it as completely natural that even families with two parents of the same sex have children. All children, regardless of the family constellation, must be guaranteed a good childhood.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e1044	Today, the Stortinge can finally debate and decide how the biotechnology field should be regulated in the future. I want to thank the committee for doing a good job with the setting. However, this debate has been delayed for ten years. The red-green government was supposed to have evaluated the law in 2008, but was unable to do so during its eight years in government. The Solberg government delivered today's big-time message, which is being debated, in its first period. Some have criticised that the notification does not conclude in all questions and that it is left to Stortinget to conclude. I do not agree with that criticism. The communication raises all issues with arguments for and against, and it provides a good basis for debate in the United Kingdom. Some of the themes are quite correct that the government has not concluded. It's simply because we're completely open that the two parties that then put in government had different views on many of these areas. I believe that big business politicians are able both to debate and to conclude for themselves, although many of these issues are ethically difficult. Biotechnology offers many opportunities for human benefit, but it is the Stortinget that should determine where the ethical boundaries are to go for the opportunities offered by technology. I agree with the majority of the Committee that this limit should be assessed in our view on ethics and values in Norway, regardless of what may be permitted in other countries. I am particularly pleased that the Committee insists that the aim of the law is to continue to be to make use of biotechnology for the benefit of people in a society where there is room for everyone. It is also good to see that most of the government's proposals have received broad support in the committee. This will include, inter alia: strengthen the legal status of children by reducing the age limit for the right to know who is the donor from 18 to 15 years of age, by giving parents the obligation to inform their children that they have been made by donation, and by improving the rules for assessing the suitability of couples seeking assisted fertilisation, inter alia. by introducing an obligation to provide a child care certificate. It is also good, and in a high time, that women with medical conditions that may lead to infertility at an early age are entitled to retain unfertilised eggs. I am pleased that the majority of the Committee supports the government's proposal to allow genetic testing of fertilised eggs in some cases, even if the applicant does not wish to be shown carrier status for, among others. Huntington's disease, which is a serious hereditary disease. The few couples involved should be able to get help to have children while not knowing whether they are likely to develop a serious heritable disease for which there is no treatment option. It is good that the majority of the Committee supports the proposal to harmonise and simplify today's rules on gene therapy. The rules will be easier to practice while maintaining strict regulation of the field. I am also pleased that the majority of the Committee supports the proposal to provide clear rules on when the Biotechnology Act applies to research involving predictive genetic research, i.e. tests to show whether you have a particular genetic defect that will increase the risk of disease during your life. Assisted fertilisation for solitude and egg donation are two questions that have been much discussed. A majority of the Committee supports this. However, I am surprised that a majority in the committee consider allowing embryo donation, i.e. the donation of fertilised eggs. Embryo donation is not recommended by a majority of the Biotechnology Council. I believe that embryo donation only takes into account the needs of adults. The child will have no genetic connection with any of the parents. I think it can be called into question whether this violates the purpose of the law to exploit biotechnology with respect for human rights and personal integrity. It is not relevant to argue that adopted children do not grow up with their genetic parents. The purpose of adoption is quite different and meets the needs of a born child for parents. I would also like to stress the importance of early ultrasound not being introduced as an offer to all pregnant women. Early ultrasound for all healthy pregnant women is not recommended by the Institute of Public Health. There is no evidence that this will bring health benefits to both mother and child. In addition, routine early ultrasound is not closely related to the purpose of the law that biotechnology should be exploited for the benefit of people in a society where there is ‘space for all’. As is well known, an offer of early ultrasound for all healthy women will lead to fewer children being born with e.g. Down syndrome. I have also noticed that the majority of the Committee proposes that restrictions on the use of donor semen be linked to the number of families per donor and not to the number of children per donor. I believe that this proposal will work against its purpose. The proposal gives some families the opportunity to get a larger sibling with the same donor. I do not necessarily agree that this should be given priority over sperm donation to as many childless couples as possible. The proposal may also lead to fewer children being born through sperm donation, because the total number of children being born will depend on how many children each family can have. In my opinion, therefore, the government's proposal in the communication to increase the number of children per donor from eight to twelve, divided into up to six families, is more appropriate. The proposal will meet both the family's needs for siblings from the same donor and provide an offer to as many children as possible. It can also be assessed whether the restriction of 12 children per donor can be waived when it is important for the individual family. This can help more children in one family to be biological siblings. However, it is important that this is further investigated in order to ensure that there is no risk that the half-sibling will have children without being familiar with its genetic kinship. I have followed and will, of course, follow this debate carefully, and I will follow up the decision of the Stortinge after today's discussion. In some areas, there will be a need for professional investigations. There may also be prima facie clarifications that remain after Storting's treatment. We will prepare a legislative amendment as soon as possible, which, of course, will have to be consulted in general before it is promoted by the Stortinget.

They see it as a victory, and the doctors see it as a wonder that Magnus still lives and can even go himself. But the family also knows that Magnus can at any time be hit by a severe epidemic that makes him bedridden, or can kill him. Anne is the head of the Mitokondrie Association in Norway and fights to prevent other women from transmitting such serious diseases to their future children. Mitocondrine is inherited from mother, but now the researchers have found a way to replace these mitochondrines that do not work, in the eggshell of mother, and replace them with fresh cells from a donor egg. The Labour Party, together with the Progress Party and the Socialist Left, wants this treatment to become legal in Norway and that our professionals should be able to follow developments in the countries in which this treatment is being carried out. We have come to the conclusion that saying that this should be illegal in Norway means defending that children are born with a known, serious illness when we know that it is possible to do something about it. The mitochondria is called the power plant of the cells. When they are not working properly, severe failure of energy-intensive organs such as the heart, nervous system and muscles occurs. The earlier you develop the disease, the worse the symptoms get for those who are affected. There is very little to use from this donor egg shell for a mitochondria donation. The very nucleus of the cell remains untouched. It's in the cell nucleus at 99.99 pst. of the DNA of man, that is, all genetic properties. The child still inherits Mom's freckles and Dad's curls. Claiming that this gives children three parents is a misunderstanding. The United Kingdom, as mentioned in the debate, has debated this thoroughly and opened up the method because it safely prevents genetic disease that can cause children pain, suffering and early death. The first children to be born during mitochondria donation are expected to be born in England later this year. This is a development of the Norwegian professional community should follow and contribute to Norway. In the Workers' Party, we are pleased that the Socialist Left Party and the Advance Party also contribute to a majority to explore the possibility of helping these families.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e1568	The State Guidelines were established by the Ministry of Industry and Fisheries with effect from 13 February 2015. I have stated in the business meeting that the guidelines will apply both to regional health businesses and to health businesses. Today’s guidelines replace the guidelines laid down on 31 March 2011. They continued the principle that management wages should be competitive but not pay-leading. Another important step taken at the same business meeting was to adopt the amendments to the statutes. It was clarified that health regions are required to draw up an annual statement of salary and other remuneration for the Executive Director and other senior staff. The declaration shall be included in the annual accounts and therefore also be publicly available. It is the responsibility of the Management Board to employ the Executive Director and to establish the entity’s framework for the remuneration of senior management. The agreement concluded shall be in line with the guidelines of the State, competitive but non-remunerated. Moderation shall be displayed. The principles of national guidelines do not provide a facilitator of what is the right level of pay. Compared with the corresponding other state-owned companies, so-called categories 3 and 4, the salaries of the managing directors in the health regions are not paid in any way. According to the Guidelines, reasonable termination payments may be agreed for senior executives. The termination fee should not be used when the manager himself has initiated the termination. Termination and pay during the period of notice should also not exceed 12 months’ remuneration. In practice, however, many health business agreements are limited to 12 months. Legal positions are not directly addressed in the State guidelines. When health business boards recruit managers internally, these managers often have to interrupt clinical careers. Their closest local job option is exactly the same health care provider. They then need a guarantee to go back to clinical work if they do not succeed as leaders. It is important that the boards have sufficient room for action to recruit good managers. Good managers are an important prerequisite for success — both in relation to their own employees and in terms of providing services to the population. In the light of the issues raised in the last few months, I would like to ask regional health companies to review their practices in the field of final agreements and the use of legal remedies.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e1753	Demographic developments with an increasing number of older people in the population will characterise the picture of the disease and the need for healthcare in the decades to come. Without any change, we may experience a sharp increase in resource needs in the coming years, as the national health and hospital plan forecasts show. The representative is concerned about the appropriate capacity for day care in hospitals. There are a lot of factors that affect a future bed requirement. This makes it difficult to estimate a precise figure for the required bed capacity. Demographic developments in the elderly and more people living with complex disorders may increase the need for bed capacity. At the same time, new treatment options, changes in organisation and better logistics could be counterproductive and reduce the need for daily treatment. It is the task of health regions to plan the future service provision. As a follow-up to the National Health and Hospital Plan, I commissioned them to develop regional development plans. These plans shall include, inter alia: show possible changes in task-sharing, capacity needs, organisation and operating economy – and their consequences. A model has been developed for predicting the activity and capacity needs of hospitals used by all health businesses in the work on development plans and planning of new hospitals. The model shall take into account, in addition to demographic projections, disease development, medical technological development, patient expectations, expected day-to-day transition and use of observation beds and patient hotels. When the regional development plans are presented in 2018, we will for the first time have a comprehensive picture of service development and overall capacity needs in Norway. These plans will also provide an important basis for the next national health and health plan presented in 2019. A number of investments have been made in new hospital buildings in large parts of the country and further development is being undertaken to a large extent in order to meet future challenges. In the past, hospitals were built to an even greater extent than they are today based on the need for bed capacity. However, in recent years, there has been strong growth in outpatient activity and day care. New, gentle treatments allow patients to be discharged from hospitals more quickly. In addition, technological developments facilitate the decentralisation of specialist health services and more patient-related treatment. It is good for patients, and it will also affect the future need for bed capacity. The development of St. Olav's hospital can illustrate this. Health Mid-Norway has informed that when planning new St. Olav's hospital, there was great uncertainty and discussion related to the dimensioning of beds. In 2003, the hospital had 889 beds. It was decided to build 770 beds in the new university hospital. In addition, it was arranged for a possible extension with 72 beds. Today, St. Olav's hospital uses 722 beds. It is less than the lowest estimate in the planning process and 184 less than in 2003. The activity at St. Olav's hospital has increased significantly since then – with fewer beds. New hospitals are adapted to developments in medical technology and are designed to rapidly implement new methods of investigation and treatment. Furthermore, it facilitates the utilisation of bed capacity across sections and flexibility in construction, and it is organised for building. In some hospitals, it is possible to re-disposal rooms used for other purposes — for example: offices and patient hotels – for bed rooms, as a spare capacity. Providing greater flexibility in this way in combination with new organisation provides more effective patient logistics, resulting in better use and increased capacity in hospitals. Akershus University Hospital has done a major improvement work on optimising operations in the last year. Activity has increased significantly over the same period due to population growth. At the same time, there are now fewer Corridor Asians in the health business than in many years. By using experienced doctors in the emergency department, acutely ill patients get a more accurate diagnosis at an earlier stage, and treatment starts faster at the right place. This reduces the duration of treatment and thus the length of bedtime and the need for bed places. The new Eastfold Hospital has had a high number of Corridor Asians after the opening, and they have faced challenges at the emergency reception that the hospital is now working to repair. The experience from Eastfold, Akershus and other hospitals shows that it takes time before operations are adapted to new hospital concepts and the benefits can be taken out. Good health care for an increasing population of older people is an important issue for the government. Most people want to live an active and meaningful life in their own homes and in the longest release of hospitalization. That is why we have just launched the reform Life throughout our lives, where activity, food and health are at the centre. Prevention is as is known the best medicine to avoid disease and reduce the need for specialist health services. Furthermore, we are committed to improving the provision of municipal health services, where we will try out primary health teams and follow-up teams. Services that lead to closer follow-up of chronic sick and elderly with a complex need for services will contribute to past efforts and prevent disease development. One of the objectives is to reduce the need for hospitalisation. Many of these patients have long hospital stays, and many have unwanted cleaning. The use of beds in municipal emergency units and intermediate beds is a good offer for these patients. It may also reduce the need for bed capacity in hospitals. The new national health and health plan shall be submitted during 2019. The objective will be to implement the patient’s healthcare in a sustainable way. In order to achieve this, the rate of conversion needs to be increased, which we are doing well. The identification and analysis of unjustified variation in consumption, quality and capacity utilisation facilitates local improvement efforts by the service. Improvement of workflow, logistics and streamlining of patient pathways, e.g. related to studies and trial responses, is good for the patient and could reduce bedtime and needs. We are all engaged in realising a healthcare service that has the necessary capacity. The response to the necessary capacity depends on many factors that need to be seen in the context. We're getting more, and we're getting older. It requires increased capacity. At the same time, technologies and better work processes allow patients to be treated more quickly and to a greater extent outside hospitals. There is not one figure that answers what is the right capacity – either today or in the future. The most important thing is that we have good processes to plan and convert – at all levels, from the Storting to the individual hospital ward. In any case, all patients should feel safe and safe when they need treatment and follow-up, including in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e1840	I would like to start by thanking the members of the Committee for a good and constructive cooperation. The Committee delivers a unanimous opinion on the Stortinget. In this management report, the National Audit Office has focused on the extent to which data from national health registries are used to achieve health policy objectives. Health records store health information and are a generic term for both statutory health records and medical quality records. The medical health records are established to provide systematic evidence of treatment outcomes. The statutory health records shall perform nationwide tasks such as health surveillance, prevention, preparedness, financing and health care management. The background to the study has been that some health records do not have sufficient data quality and that there is a risk that information from the registers will not be used to achieve their purpose. There are many actors involved in the work of health registers, and there is a risk that work is not well managed and coordinated. The investigation period is the years from 2015 to April 2017. The following are the points made in the report: Many of the registers have data that is little used. Many health records have not sought quality and are not suitable for comparisons over time. Regional health establishments do not meet the national requirements for registers. Norway lacks a health record for several major disease areas, such as Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders, mental disorders and psychosis. The Ministry of Health and Welfare’s use of instruments has not been adapted to health records’ objectives and challenges. It is important that the health records have the quality necessary to capture disease cases and compare trends over time. We need good medical quality records to document treatment outcomes systematically and to know which treatments have the best effect. Such data are an important basis for quality improvements and for research. Regional health establishments are responsible for the establishment, operation and financing of the national quality registers. The establishment of new national medical records depends on the local initiative and the agreement of all regional health establishments. The report states that since 2016 one of the health regions has been opposed to the approval of all applications for new national quality records, with the consequence that no applications have been submitted to the Health Directorate, even if the health care records consider it necessary. Such a practice cannot continue. The Committee agrees with the National Audit Office’s strong recommendation that health businesses should address this issue and establish a system to address the need for national medical quality records across health regions. The Committee also supports better coordination and cooperation between health regions on the national medical quality records, in order to achieve the objectives of the registers. The report refers to major deficiencies or uncertainties in 26 of the 64 examined records that capture all cases of disease. In one third of the registers, own data are not considered to be sufficiently up-to-date and in nearly half of the statutory registers, the data are not considered suitable for comparisons over time. What also emerges is that there are no health records for several major diseases, such as musculoskeletal disorders, mental disorders and drug addiction. These diseases are the main cause of disability and falling out of work. Therefore, health data and good medical quality records are particularly important in these health areas in order to identify the effects of treatment and follow-up. It is also important to be able to make comparisons within their own country, and also across borders, for example. Here in the North, where we live quite equal lives. The Committee asks the State Councils to take seriously the findings of the report and to follow up the recommendations of the National Audit Office. The development of new health records, the improvement of existing and the closure of out-of-date health records must be a priority and a continuous work. In its observations, the Committee calls on the Government to inform the Storting of this work annually in an appropriate manner.
