ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e1893	I am referring to the fact that the Ministry of Health and Health has taken a clear grip since 2016 on the strategic management of the health registry field. Furthermore, the Executive Board for eHealth from 2017 has been responsible for monitoring and coordinating the development work through the Health Data Programme. The central tasks of the Health Data Programme are the development and use of common solutions for registers, the establishment of a health analysis platform, harmonisation of codes and terminology and the contribution to the further development of legislation. The Programme shall contribute to better privacy and information security, better availability and increased use of data, better data quality and more efficient registry management. I would like to stress that through the role of national authority and through the national eHealth management system, the Directorate for eHealth has introduced the health registry field of new tools and new competences, which provide stronger development and which contribute to new solutions and better coordination in the future. The health record field is subject to ordinary account management and ownership management. The governance dialogue reflects the new priorities through mission, management parameters and performance requirements. The Directorate for eHealth has prepared a separate governance document for the Health Data Programme. The Directorate has also been entrusted with the task of investigating and proposing a model for national portfolio management of health registry measures. Overall, I believe that these measures contribute to better and more effective management of the health register field. I am concerned that more use of health data can bring great benefits to the individual patient and to the Norwegian healthcare system. In today’s system, access to health records data for quality improvement, health analysis and research can be both resource- and time-consuming. This was the background for the establishment of the Health Data Committee by the Ministry in June 2016. The Committee has proposed legal, organisational and technical measures for easier and safer access to health data. Several of the recommendations are followed up by the Health Data Programme and the development of a health analysis platform. Health regions are responsible for developing national medical quality records in areas where there is a need for knowledge. In 2017 and 2018 I have commissioned them to: develop a plan for the development and establishment of new medical quality records in priority disciplines; ensure a governance model that promotes quality records and contributes to the development of new cross-regional medical quality records; draw up an electronic status report for the national medical quality records with the aim of achieving the coverage and use of the quality records; The Health Directorate decides which medical quality records are to have national status. Each year, the registers shall be assessed in accordance with the criteria laid down and the Directorate shall decide whether the registers continue to qualify for national status. Continuous efforts are being made to improve the quality of national medical quality records. In the strategy for work on medical quality records for 2016-2020, health regions have set concrete efficacy targets related to coverage, the provision of results to data reporting entities, the presentation of results from clinical improvement work and the presentation of patient-reported data. In 2017, the results of 48 national medical quality records were published on quality registers.no. I would also like to use financial incentives to increase the coverage of the quality registers, and I would like to put in place a separate regulation on medical quality records that could have an impact on coverage and quality. As regards the need for better data on major disease areas, I believe it is right to give priority to the development of an infrastructure of health registers that overall will provide a better data base. Firstly, it is about prioritising the development of key key registers, such as municipal patient and user registers, which are established this year, the further development of the Norwegian patient register and the Cause of Death Register, and the development of a directly identifiable pharmaceutical register – based on the current Register of Recipes. Secondly, it is about making it easy and secure to compile data across existing registers and data sources. In this way, we can get better data in large disease areas, such as: Psychiatric disorders and disorders without entering the path of new registers. Further development of the health register field is continuous work. The Government will therefore inform the Government each year about the status and progress of the State through an overall review in the budget proposal of the Ministry of Health and Welfare.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e1958	I would like to thank the members of the Committee for their good cooperation in this matter as well. The aim of this study has been to assess whether the authorities facilitate a good referral practice from the stipulators to the specialist healthcare service, so that the resources in this area are used well enough. The referral practices of decision-makers have a significant impact on the activities of hospitals. Norwegian healthcare is organised according to the LEON principle, i.e. The Lavest Effective Care Level Principle, which means that a greater part of the patient’s course should take place in the primary healthcare service. According to the Riksrevision report, much indicates that today’s referral practices are not working optimally and that healthcare resources are being wasted. More than every four regular laymen regularly refer patients to specialist healthcare even if they do not expect any medical benefit from it. At the same time, every third hospital doctor acknowledges that they regularly include patients for investigation even if the referral indicates that the patient should not have been referred to. This violates the LEON principle. The survey covers 650 hospital doctors who have evaluated and prioritised referrals in selected disciplines from 2014 to 2017 and nearly 700 permanent practitioners who have answered each of their questionnaires. The report shows that many decision-makers and hospital doctors do not cooperate well enough on the referrals. There is often a different understanding of what is necessary information in a referral. Reference is made to large variations in the number of references between municipalities and between permanent agencies. It is not the municipality size, the central location of the municipality, or the proportion of older people who can explain the differences. Both the medics and the hospital doctors say they lack tools for simple electronic dialogue with each other. The national audit suggests that the introduction of digital dialogue messages will hopefully improve the quality of referrals. It points to unclear responsibilities between primary and specialised health services, especially in the case of mentally ill and those with drug problems. The Council of State says that he takes this into account with the highest seriousness, and that new models for the interaction and organisation of the services offered to this patient group should be tested through different pilot projects, such as: municipal operation of DPS, district psychiatric centres, testing of follow-up teams, etc. The Committee believes that this is a good thing. People with mental or drug problems often require services from both levels of administration at the same time. That is why good interaction is important here. The National Audit Office considers it important to involve the decision-makers in the development and concreteisation of cooperation agreements between municipalities and health businesses. The report states that the Council of State does not agree with the Riksrevision's claim that the municipality's care-for-responsibilities towards the decision-makers and their referral are unclear. The Council of State refers to the provisions of Section 7 in conjunction with Section 24 which gives the municipalities a clear responsibility to ensure good referral practices by their decision-makers. Although it is so clear in the regulation, the Committee feels that this responsibility needs to be clarified and that the Ministry should support the municipalities in this work, so that the intention of the Storting can be fulfilled. The study shows that the referral guide is not known to the permanent doctors. Only half know the content. The National Audit Service assumes that the quality of referrals to the specialist health service will improve when the supervisor is in place and implemented in the medical records systems of the decision-makers. A unanimous committee expects the Council of State and the Ministry to take the report seriously and to follow up the recommendations of the Riksrevision. It is important that the municipalities understand what is in their care-for-responsibility regulations, and that good follow-up systems are facilitated. Municipalities need to know what measures they can take in their follow-up to their decision-makers. Health enterprises also need to take their share of responsibility and systematise information on the quality of referrals made by the local authorities in order to be able to pass them on to local authorities and the local authorities. It is recommended that good systems be developed for regular feedback to the decision-makers on their own practices and those of others. The National Audit Service recommends that the Ministry should clarify the responsibilities of patients in the field of mental health protection, and in this context examine how the specialist healthcare service can best provide training and guidance, so that the local health service is better able to provide a good offer to this patient group, and ensure that healthcare companies and municipalities clarify what conditions actually give the right to specialist healthcare for this patient group. Finally, I would like to make a small comment on the matter. The National Audit Office's report is about findings and recommendations. The majority of the Committee, the Labour Party, the Centre Party and the SV, have expressed their views on health policy in a separate comment on the cause of the findings. This has chosen the Committee's minority, the Right, the Advance Party and the Christian People's Party, not to comment because we believe it does not belong in the consideration of this report.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e2053	In this article, I do not wish to go into the individual proposals to which the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs has commented, but I would like to start by stressing that the Storting has, through its operation, a picture of instructions to the government, and that the government is obliged to implement its decisions as far as it can. If a single Council of State or Government fails to fulfil its obligations in accordance with its decisions, it may and should have some serious consequences. If this important principle in our system is broken, I think we will destroy what is meant by the Constitution, namely that the power of the people is to be exercised through the elected assemblies. At the same time, it is quite clear that the government has a responsibility to ensure that the Stortinget guide and give the Stortinget the information necessary for the Stortinget to take the most appropriate decisions. It's not like all these decisions come to the table on the day that Stortinget makes a decision. As a rule, decisions are the result of a relatively long process, where the government and the members of the government are also aware of what is going on. It therefore requires actual members of the government to take note of it in the Stortinget, in the Grandningssalalen, if, in their opinion, a decision cannot be implemented, as the Storting has ambitions. Often, political victories are celebrated when proposals are passed in the hall. The degree to which decisions are actually followed up is often less noticeable. Report. St. 7, which we have here, represents an important reminder and provides a good summary of the state of implementation of the individual decisions. The fact that more than one hundred decisions taken in this room in the period 2015–2016 are still not implemented builds up under the need for someone to systematically examine how Stortinget’s call decisions are followed up. Some decisions may not, however, be implemented so easily. For these cases, there is reason to stress that there is only one way to rewrite a decision. It is by the Storting itself making a different decision, or by the Storting itself interpreting how the decision is to be implemented. So if the government doesn't find it possible to implement a decision The Storting has ordered the government to implement, there's actually an obligation on the government to come back to the Storing and try to invite the Storing to make another decision. Let me stress that there is reason to be concerned about the strong increase in the number of instruction decisions that we have received in recent years. It is quite obvious that the large amount with a decision point – a total of 459 in the period 2016–2017 – allows questions to be asked about whether the balance between accumulation and government is actually being rocked. When a government does not have a sufficiently strong parliamentary basis in the Stortinget, it will of course be possible for the Stortinget to gather a number of decisions across the proposals of the government. The danger is that there will be many such ‘request decisions’, or appeal decisions, as I would call it – i.e. decisions taken by the Stortinget and signaled that such a decision would, but not implemented. If there's too many in this category, I'm afraid that in the long run, this could lead to people losing control of politics and our people's regime. In my view, some of the appeal decisions that Stortinget has taken could, with the advantage of our wording as an "exploratory decision", or a decision of distribution, which should be referred to as a fairer measure in my case. There will then be decisions in which the government is asked to settle questions, put them into a larger context and return to the Stortinget either with proposals or with big messages in the understanding of the signals given by Stortinget. Looking forward to looking for a better practice, there is reason to wonder if the Stortinge may also be more aware of the way we take call decisions, what kind of decisions we take, and what direction we give them. In a longer time perspective, we should also discuss the relationship between magnificence and government and make sure that we set the situation straight so that the government actually dares say no when the government thinks it, and not pretend that it is, and then come back and try to erase the quality of the decision that is taken. Report. St. 7 on call and distribution decisions, which we have to deal with today, covers only the periods 2015-2016 and 2016-2017, and thus says nothing about any previous stays. The Committee therefore asked the Prime Minister for an overview of non-implemented decisions from autumn 2013 to spring 2015. The letter of reply from the Prime Minister states that 27 decisions from this period are still not followed up. Finally, let me repeat what I started with: there must be no doubt that once the Storting has taken an appeal decision, the government has to stick to this. If you don't want to, you'll get to Stortinget and ask the Stortinge to make a different decision. Finally, a explanations of vote: The Labour Party cannot vote for the proposal of the Central Party, SV and the Christian People's Party as it is now formulated, where it is proposed that the Stortinget ask the Government to base the proposal for a representative in Document 8:123 S for 2016-2017 on the follow-up to the decision-making process 1035,1036, 1037, 1038, 1039 and 1040. If the proposers change the proposal for the adoption of the decision to be taken, the Workers' Party will be able to support this.

I do not want to draw up any request decisions here, but I see that it remains to be confirmed, among other things. issues relating to the follow-up to police reform, the follow-up to the violence field and some issues in the foreign field. In the government's proposal for the 2019 government budget, I would like to return to the follow-up to new request decisions and individual request decisions that have not been sufficiently followed up. It is important for me to stress the decision of the Storting to be followed up and reported back to the Storting as quickly as possible in an appropriate manner.

The previous committee chose not to deal with this matter, and one of the reasons for it was all the recordings that came, not least through the hearings. The previous committee decided to postpone the examination of this matter, and therefore this family and cultural committee has been the one who has tried to solve this problem ashore. We have carried out new consultations in this context, and almost as many hearing bodies have chosen to participate now. I would like to say a little about the different paragraphs that were at least mentioned in the hearings. I'll start with Section 67. This paragraph provides that authors shall not be regarded as having transferred a more extensive right than that clearly stated in the contract, which is referred to as the specialty principle. A law-making of the specialty principle implies a clarification intended to strengthen the position of creative and performing artists in unclear contractual relationships. This is a paragraph with which the Committee fully agrees when it comes to references and comments. Then we have Section 69, which was also brought forward in the hearing, right to reasonable remuneration. More input was received, including: a statement that there may be a need to establish a dispute resolution. I am pleased that a Joint Committee requests the Government to investigate a dispute resolution mechanism for setting reasonable remuneration and to return to the Storting on a case-by-case basis. This was an important input from several hearing bodies, to which the Committee has listened, and so it is an overall committee that asks the government to do so. So to § 71: That's the paragraph it's been the most noise around. Although the Ministry only intended to codify the applicable law and that the legal status was not changed, several of the hearing bodies were negative. Among other things, the authors feared that the negotiating position was weakened, and that one could be pressured to transfer rights to an extent that was not desirable. After an overall assessment, the Committee has then chosen not to promote the proposal for Section 71 of the Proposition, but we would like to be clear from the Committee's point of view that the law in force should not be interpreted as amended in the light of the fact that the proposal from the Proposition to Section 71 is withdrawn. Then I would like to say a little about Section 43, the so-called class room rule, which after much discussion is now included in the law as originally proposed. At the same time, Stortinget will take two different request decisions relating to this paragraph, one proposed by a Joint Committee and one of the opposition parties. Paragraph 43 implies that not only traditional transmission but also Internet transmission is defined to be within the private area when it occurs in the class. The proposers believe that the consequences of this non-reimbursable flow are deficiently investigated, and we welcome the government's review of both economic and other issues. We are concerned that the rightholders will in the long term lose revenue because of a change in technology, and ask the government to investigate and also consider what changes to Section 43 are necessary in order to establish a licensing system. It has been unbureaucratic, it has made the money go to those who have copyright, and we are ready to make changes if it turns out that the opening that is now taking place is damaging these objectives that we have described. This literally means that you have to map all of the rights that have been transferred to the film. Finally, we also have a split setting for this with the classroom scheme, the class room rule. We now see that instead of having a sample production in a classroom, it is usually directly digital from the web through streaming and other schemes – a very good and broad use of different mental works in teaching. It's something that an overall committee seems to be positive. It gives a good and varied teaching. But once again, we have to make sure that those who have the copyright are paid. That's why we ask the government to look at how streaming can also come under the collective arrangements, and we ask the government to come back with this. It is fully possible to do this in a very unbureaucratic way that ensures that teachers in classrooms can still use a variety of different kinds of mental works in teaching and at the same time ensure that, through the collective arrangements that we know well from the analogue world, those who have created what is being used are paid. In the end, we say that where there is doubt, this doubt is going to benefit the author, because it has to be clear what is being passed on. I think there's a good balance between maybe strong commercial interests on the manufacturer's side and artists who may not have the legal competence. But it has to be at the same time that this is a two-party collaboration. We have found in the setting that exists a good balance for both sides of the world's ability to create spiritism even in the future.

We believe it is an important principle that everyone who does a job should be paid for the job they do, and this also applies to artists. This means that there is an enormous gap in value, where large global players make a huge contribution to the use of undeclared copyright. The proposal for a new EU Copyright Directive proposes to impose on network services that store and provide access to large quantities of user-loaded works to cooperate with rightholders in order to prevent unauthorised use. We believe that this is a major step towards rebalancing this crooked balance, and we believe that Norway should take up the fight with the major global companies, together with the EU and Europe, and that we are united in this to prevent the illegal use of copyright. That is why we want to wait until the EU has concluded in its work on the new copyright directive. I do not understand the allegations that a statutory act would result in a total transfer of copyright to the employer, that it was a so-called purchase paragraph. I didn't see this as the intention of the government in handing over the proposition. Rather, such an understanding should be seen as a misinterpretation of the content of the bill based on a lack of trust between workers and employers in the Norwegian cultural sector. This is in practice not a perspective shared by my party, which went out early and suggested that the paragraph be subtracted from the bill. We have done that in our treatment and made it clear in our remarks, and then we also had a party, not a protest, about the conclusion of the bill.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e2536	Today we are going to deal with a long extension and absolutely necessary road project, rv. 555 Sotra compound in Hordaland. Work on the link started in 1999. Today's Sotra-bru was opened in 1972 and was then built for the future. It has a capacity of 12000 ÅDT, today the load is 28 000 ÅDT. With today's bridge as the only access to the municipalities of Fjell, Sund and Eyegarden – with an extensive business life – the infrastructure is very vulnerable. With an annual traffic of about 27 400 vehicles, there is a major queue problem for public transport as well. In the period 2005–2016 there were a total of 143 personal injury accidents, six of which were fatal accidents. This is serious. The project includes the construction of 9,4 km of four-field road, with a new four-field bridge with a separate walk and cycle path. Also three smaller bridges are part of the project, as well as walking and cycling routes. The project also facilitates the prioritisation of public transport between terminals. An external quality assurance of the project has been carried out, and the KS2 consultant and the State Road Administration are largely in agreement with the development costs and prerequisites for the implementation of the project. Since quality assurance in 2008, the project has had a significant increase in costs, which is in fact 70 pst. Converted to 2018 level, the development costs have now been estimated to be approx. 10 B.C.E. kr. Socio-economic net benefits are calculated to be SEK 2.1 billion. kr. The project has been treated locally in all the municipalities concerned and in the district of Hordaland. Hordaland County Municipality has also adopted a guarantee for the project. A two-way recovery of tolls has been made at both new and existing Sotra bridges for a 20-year period. The average income per pass must be 40,50 kroner with the assumptions otherwise made. It is stipulated in the case that the Ministry of Transport is empowered to adjust tariffs in such a way that the average tariff is as provided for in the proposal. Hordaland County Municipality and Bergen Municipality have decided that detailed ceiling stops will be taken into consideration as it approaches the opening of the road system. The other municipalities have decided that a zero-emission vehicle should pay half of the normal fare for tariff group 1, in line with national guidelines. The national road system refers to the fact that if the introduction of a half tariff for a zero-emission vehicle is not sufficient to maintain the average tariff, the tariffs for all vehicles may be adjusted. By decision of the Ministry of Transport of Storting, the Ministry of Transport shall be empowered to adjust the tariffs in such a way that the average tariff becomes as provided for in this Protocol. The proposal to adjust the tariffs accordingly requires the consent of the county municipality as a guarantor. The big concern is how the next seven years will be before the project is completed. The OPS model is, in our opinion, a good instrument in this respect. With this as a rear carpet, it is interesting to record the continuing resistance of the Workers' Party to renewal through the use of e.g. public-private cooperation. In the setting, I read that the Workers' Party is still asserting its opposition to private financing of public road projects. One might at any rate say that the resistance has been consistent over time, you ask me, for October 26, 2011, the then prime minister from the Workers' Party answered a question about road construction from Knut Arild Hareide. He said, ‘If Norway starts to finance road projects, we have arranged very foolishly. Our problem is not that we have too little money, our problem is the total use of money and possibly pressure on the interest rate. We don't have to buy roads on payment, we can give them cash." If the Workers' Party still believes that borrowing is a wrong instrument in the road sector, there is reason to recall that the total loan amount that the red-green government was behind in order to build a new road was greater than ever before. The tolling system has interest charges that companies have to meet as a result of the loans they raise in ordinary banks. The companies also have other costs of operating the toll solutions. In addition, toll companies must advance their contribution to the construction of the road and therefore receive interest. If the Workers' Party still believes that this is setting itself up very stupidly, I think you have to do the calculations again. The working party provided to the greatest extent significant borrowings, or ‘roads of payment’, as they call it. There's a paradox. There is little credibility as you constantly express your resistance to, for example, OPS models, but otherwise accept that you are constantly collecting more money through borrowing. I am most concerned about this matter that we will get a faster expansion, lower risk and more money. We believe that the OPS model contributes to it. The most important thing now is to get built. 555, and that's a majority for here in this room. It will be built, and when the project is finished, it will mean a lot to the whole region. I am pleased, and I am pleased that the project has now come to Stortinget and can be implemented. The Christian People's Party supports the majority position.

These are absolutely central questions, and these are questions we have to take into account on site by location by location. We have to make some choices. We have to be tough in the merge if we're going to do something about these environmental issues we're facing. We have to! I am absolutely sure that those who need the car, from the districts and in – I have been living in the district for many years – can access that road if all those who do not need to take the car can take collective means of transport. Then they are removed from that path. If we make more and more car traffic and make it easier and easier for the drivers, we are in the old, good trap again. It's easy to have a position that the representative Johnsen has, it's delicious. Had I stood here then, I might have received local support – that's great what he says – and I could use colorful words about several fields, as Johnsen does. But he doesn't think long-term. If we are to take some responsibility for this globe, we need to see each case separately, but also together. It is the overall load, the overall mindset that spews out a new direction for Norway — in terms of transport, and in terms of greenhouse gas emissions. The last alarming messages from the Arctic came today and yesterday, about what it looks like around Svalbard. There's a relationship between urban growth agreements, how we solve the Sotra connection, how we solve the transport problems in the Drammen, and how the polar bear is doing on Svalbard. It sounds distant, it might sound naive, but it's actually not. There is a connection – and I don't think about the Sotra connection – between all these individual issues and the climate turmoil we're facing. I ask that all these individual matters be seen in context, and I also ask for respect for such a view. It's so easy to label us -- and on each other in this room, like we're not responsible enough, or we're naive, a little far away from reality. I'm not!

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e2960	At the request of the Transport and Communications Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 5 minutes for each party group and 5 minutes for members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e2969	This matter concerns two proposals for representatives. One is that the planning of ports should be with the Coastal Works, and the other is that the fishing ports should continue to be a State responsibility. The proposals have a background in Prop. 84 S for 2016-2017, which concerns the re-division of the regionally elected level, proposing that responsibility for the fishing ports be transferred from the State to the regional level from 1 January 2020. The size joined this, cf. Inner. 385 S for 2016-2017. The proposal promoted by the Centre Party is reversing the decision to transfer to the local population-elected level, while the proposal promoted by the Workers' Party deals with adaptation to the Storting decision. The government has justified the transfer of fishing ports to the regions with a desire to strengthen local democracy by transferring more power and authority to local/regional elected levels, and that the development of fishing ports naturally falls within the role of county municipalities as community developers. It has been pointed out that it may be a challenge for each region to develop competences similar to those of the Coastal Plant today. In today's system, the Swedish Coastal Agency is responsible for project planning, while the actual development is being put to tender. Many of the technical studies carried out under the auspices of the Coastal Administration in the planning process are currently purchased services. The coastal works shall complete the planning of projects to be carried out under their own control until 2020 and shall also implement all projects starting before that date. In addition to this, the Coastal Agency has a portfolio of fishing port projects within the national transport plan 2018-2029, which are in different planning phases. Commenting on the proposals, the Ministry of Transport stated that project continuity can be ensured by giving the county councils and municipalities access to the coastal planning material as it exists today, and thus will not have to start planning completely again where the coastal works have already started. Furthermore, the Ministry states that, in order to facilitate the realisation of new projects, county municipalities may start following up administrative measures to support the 2020 fisheries port financing. The coastal works will engage in dialogue with the county municipalities and will be open to information meetings and processes to facilitate the administration of county municipalities in the best possible and most flexible way. The decision on the transfer of fishing ports to the regionally elected level has created some criticism and noise along the coast of Norway. The main reason is that some uncertainties have arisen about how the transfer and funding will be carried out and whether a vacuum will arise in the planning of new actions. There are also questions as to whether the regionally elected level will be able to acquire this competence in time. The Ministry is now working on these challenges. Finally, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation. The plural position of the Committee is that the proposals are not adopted, and I expect the parties themselves to present their proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e2992	The Workers' Party has submitted a Document 8 proposal to maintain planning competence in the Coastal Works related to the planning of fishing ports. The government and the majority of the population are destroying a professional environment, knowingly and willfully. This professional environment is located in Kabelvåg and Honningsvåg, not where the regions have their administrative locations. This affects individuals, but we hear nothing about it. The Labour Party stretched out a hand to the majority to get a constructive solution to this matter, for the benefit of the coast and the people of the coast. We're recording that it wasn't interested. The majority of the handling must be pushed through – whether it destroys a professional environment or not. I can't say 'good job' to you, because here you have been neither constructive nor reasonable. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3388	First, let me thank the Committee for its good cooperation in the consideration of this proposal for a representative from several representatives of the Central Party on the State takeover of the guarantee of toll loans. It is an objective that toll financing costs should be as low as possible. Toll reform is therefore also being implemented, which will mean fewer toll companies and more efficient collection and administration. It is also worth noting that in the national transport plan 2018–2029, growth comes primarily in the form of state resources, which actually reduces the relative share of tolls. The cost of financing is an important cost element and possible cost reductions are the main argument of the proposers when they now propose that the State take over the guarantee liability for national toll loans. In its letter to the Committee, the Council of State shows that county guarantees have almost equivalent security to State guarantees, and that there is little to be recovered from cost reductions by the State taking over this guarantee liability. At the same time, we note that several county councils express concern that the total guarantee liability of the county councils gradually becomes so extensive that it may affect the county councils' overall credit rating and borrowing costs. The fact that there is now an increased guarantee liability is, of course, a positive issue – the fact that so many new ways are being built that this will be a discussion and a topic. The system of county guarantees has been a long-term practice and is established to give legitimacy to the Norwegian toll model and to ensure the best possible loan conditions. This mechanism has been in force under different policyprinciples over the years. Historically, this was probably of great importance, as some of the projects could be implemented more quickly by means of toll financing. Now we see that toll financing has become a more established part of the national transport plan, and that toll sub-financing is more planned in the construction of large road projects. In addition, a large part of what used to be national roads has been transferred to county municipalities through the administrative reform in 2010. What is now left as national roads are mainly European roads and important tribal roads. It is still important that toll-funded projects are anchored by local authorities, municipalities and counties, through specific decisions. The question is whether it is still appropriate for county councils to provide guarantees for national toll loans or whether the State itself should take that responsibility. For this reason, the majority of the Committee, consisting of the Right, the Advance Party and the Left, have asked the government to assess whether the system of county guarantees is appropriate.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3413	I would like to thank the Central Party for a good proposal. Let it be clear: toll collection, administrative and financing costs shall be as low as possible. Important reforms have been implemented to improve the efficiency of the tolling sector. However, it is also the case that several county councils express great concern about the overall guarantee liability and its possible consequences for the financing of the county's other activities. The size has decided to invest heavily on national roads and is addressing an increasing number of toll proposals on county roads. Therefore, there should be good reason for the Storting to see that the county council's concerns are real and that they need to be taken seriously. The Government Parties point out that the system of granting county guarantees for toll loans has been a long-standing practice for both county and national road projects, both to give legitimacy to the Norwegian toll model, to give local anchoring and to achieve as favourable loan conditions as possible. This is far from right, but it should not prevent us from seeing that the time now may be ripe to change this system. In the response of the Council of State to the Committee, it is pointed out that municipal and county guarantees are considered to be very safe and that no better loan conditions can be expected with State guarantees. The working party thinks this is wrong. The state has a high level of liquidity and, in the worst case, it will not have a lower loan condition than the county authorities currently have. We also believe that the local anchoring is clear enough, even in the decisions they take on local access, and that it is important that the state guarantees its own roads. I would like to mention proposal No. 1, 2 and 3 from the minority, which is here the Workers' Party and the Center Party, where we ask the government to take care of State assumes guarantee liability for existing national toll projects future toll projects provide a basis for State guarantees; and reduced borrowing costs resulting from State guarantee benefits to road users. After all, it is positive that the majority asks the government to assess whether it is appropriate for the county to continue to provide a guarantee for toll loans.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3677	Thank you to the representatives of the Left for raising an important issue, and thank you for your cooperation within the Committee in the work on this proposal for a representative. It has taken some time, simply because the Committee has had a certain need to seek information about this new technology. The proposal from the Left concerns the establishment of several smaller pilot projects using blocks to replace public databases. Blocks are a relatively new way of collecting and managing information that allows information sharing in a different way from today. Some technological environments in Norway believe that in the short term technology can provide more efficiency, better security, greater transparency and better interaction in the public sector. In the autumn of 2017, the Ministry of Municipal and Modernisation took the initiative of a study on blockbuster technology to identify the potential and potential for use of this type of technology in Norway, with a focus on the public sector. The study Distributed Truth was submitted in March 2018. The study shows that blocking technologies also have a potential in Norway, and points out that technology offers more opportunities to manage information also as a cooperation between public and private actors. Technology can provide better quality information and open up new business models that we do not know about today. As individuals, citizens can gain greater ownership and control of their own data. Many suggest that blockage technology can initially be adapted to existing and existing legislation. Privacy rules create some challenges, but most of them seem to be solvable. The majority of the Committee, the representatives of the Right, the Party of Advances and the Left, point out that the Council of State wrote in its letter of 3 April: ‘Based on the recommendations of the report, we will assess in the near future whether there is a basis for launching public sector block technology pilot projects and, if so, how best to do so. Ultimately, however, national and local authorities will have to decide whether to apply block-barring technology because it brings benefits in terms of lower costs or improved quality.’ We are pleased that the government focuses on the opportunities offered by the digitisation of public services, and also refers to the Jeleia platform, where the government will assess how blockade technologies can provide the basis for new value creation. It is important to be open to new technological solutions that can improve the provision of services to citizens and that can help to improve the efficiency of the public sector. Then it is reassuring that a study has already been carried out of the possibilities and potential of blockade technology and that this is a priority area of government. An important part of this work will be to assess the current pilot project, which the government has announced will take place in the near future. The majority of the Committee therefore sees no need to impose or take decisions in an area where the government is already well-informed. Blocking technology is exciting, and we look forward to testing this out in the public sector.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3699	«Blocks» translated from the English term «blockchain» are probably all heard of the result, but as the representative of the place said, most of us do not know the technology behind it. Technology has been developed with the intention of extracting bitcoin, but technology is suitable for much more, in fact also to solve tasks that today pose some major challenges in information sharing, verification and storage of trust-based trade and personal information. There are great opportunities for technology to strengthen privacy in an advanced technological age. Such verification has been deficient in the new, digitized world in which we live. What do I mean by that? We know, for example. that health information on the roads may constitute a breach of privacy. This also means that, for example: digital trading in properties may be hacked by outsiders. With blockade technology, there is a lot of evidence that access to public archives can be digitised without – so far – there is a risk that someone might break in and read or change the information that lies there, without legal access, or without it becoming visible that they have read it. In Norway we have not come very far in this area. Although we are at the forefront of the digitalisation of society in Norway, blockade technology has not been used to any particular extent, at least not in the public sector. The working party believes that in the public sector there is a particularly high potential for the use of this technology. Deloitte, who conducted the government investigation, even believes that blockages can lead to significant efficiency and savings in the public sector. They're actually giving an estimate of about 15 billion. kr annually, approximately 10 years in advance. This is obviously a number to which there is considerable uncertainty, but there is in any case an interesting indication of the potential. The same investigation underlined the need to facilitate extensive experimentation here in Norway. Other countries have done a part in this area, and the Labour Party believes that we should learn from the examples and follow the advice of the investigation. Therefore, we believe that it makes sense to launch smaller pilot projects using blockade technologies, for example. for public databases provided by proposers. However, it is important to make a thorough and comprehensive assessment of the best-suited databases, with a high level of emphasis on the possibility of increased safety, improved user involvement and cost savings. I would like to take up the proposal from the Labour Party, the Centre Party and the SV who are in the position, and hope that it will have a majority in the Chamber. Finally, I record that the Left has reached the peak of power when they now choose to vote against their own proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3721	I would first like to thank the President and the proposers, Trine Skei Grande, Ketil Kjeseth and Carl-Erik Grimstad, for addressing this important matter. Blocks are a relatively new technology, which opens up for the distribution of digital information, which in the universal databases can be controlled without a centralised bureaucracy. Today, as the proposers see it, there is an extensive use of databases in the Norwegian public sector, where, for example, the health sector uses around 58 000 health-related databases. The centre party is, therefore, in agreement with the proposers, which believe that blockbuster technology can be a possible solution to this challenge, but that the public has not been at the forefront when it comes to using blockbuster chains. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3734	First of all, I would like to thank the rapporteurs from the Gunnes party. It's a very important proposal. But we have to say – as many have said – that it is of course a technology that has gone under the radar of most people here in the country. It's not exactly blocked we're talking about in the lunch break or maybe over a glass of cold white wine in the summer heat, I would say. There is still considerable uncertainty about the development and potential of this technology. It's also healthy, I guess -- there should be some scepticism for such unknown technologies. Then it is good that we got the investigation from Deloitte – as others have pointed out – and that technology can have a high potential for saving and efficiency in the public sector. When it was suggested about 15 billion. kr annually – in ten years’ time – we don’t know how much it’s somehow taken out of the air, and what kind of basis they have to say it, but at least it tells something about the potential of this technology. The study found more than 100 examples internationally where attempts to use or develop these technologies in the public sector are under way, among others. Sweden. It is clear that the public sector has a particular responsibility to be in the forefront in terms of digitisation and efficiency. If the public is not obsessive about using new and cost-saving technologies where possible and reasonable, this will lead to unnecessary costs and a greater gap between private and public sector technological solutions. It says itself. It may also weaken the legitimacy of public services. It would be directly unwise not to participate in the further development of this technology, be involved in further investigation, research and testing areas in Norway where it is natural. The government should learn from the experience of the rest of the world and follow the advice given in that investigation. It is therefore appropriate to initiate smaller pilot projects using block edge technologies for public databases, for which the proposal is addressed by the proposers. The interesting thing is that blockage technology is far less advanced and more energy-intensive than other existing database technologies. Therefore, we would like to stress that blockage technology does not need to replace public databases, but can be used as a complement to different forms of mixing solutions, i.e. hybrid solutions. It is then important to make a thorough and overall assessment of the types of databases that are best suited to this type of experiment. Elements that should be included in such an assessment are the potential to increase security – which has been mentioned – the possibility to improve user involvement and cost-saving opportunities. That's what we're all after.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-15.ana.ud217e3764	Technological development is insanely exciting. It allows us to deliver both services and products, transport solutions, etc. on a much larger, better and cheaper scale than what we've dreamed of before. I started my day today at a conference, a podcast in charge of today's business, where I and the head of the Polytechnic Association, Silvija Seres, in a conversation, saw for us how drones change the future, simply by having vessels and craft that are becoming more and more automated, that can be remotely controlled or self-directed, and that solve our problems better and cheaper. It's just our own imagination that limits – and our own trust in the machines. Blockchain gets a little bit the same way. It gives us the opportunity to interact and share information, not have intermediaries to manage etc., but where everyone can participate and contribute and see what has happened. As long as you have confidence in the machine itself, you have confidence in the system. It's like a lot of people think bitcoin when you're talking about blockchain. There is at least one thing that illustrates how this works – where one does not have a central bank that sits and governs, but where everyone can be involved. You see who used the coins or the bitcoin and paid to who, and so you have a transparency in this, a decentralization of both information and transparency, which is very special. This gives possibilities -- not opportunities we should necessarily jump on just because it's exciting. We also need to see that it is useful and beneficial, but the potential is absolutely present. Therefore, the government announced earlier that a study would be conducted to look at these possibilities. The Left's proposal is a good one. I think it's natural that the Left also acts as they do, when they're actually in government and get to be in the processes that the Left in the Biggest actually asked for. So I think it's a rational explanation of this. Just because the system is really going on decentralised transparency, it would be a little weird if the state decided on some centralised, big projects. We believe that here too we should approach this in a decentralised way, simply by our different state agencies and companies looking at where you can benefit from this and start testing it out. There is a lot of experience to be gained before we fully see the potential you have in the short term, but not least in the long term. There's a lot of evidence that the technology that's at the bottom of today is not the one that's in charge tomorrow. So adding all the eggs to today's basket is not necessarily the best way to approximate it. This does not mean, however, that one should stay away from the chicken house in any way. One has to be inside to look at the possibilities, because it's just through experience we're learning from this, that's where we build up knowledge and skills that can be involved and influence what technologies are being developed, and we also have to be a good asker ourselves. The advantage in Norway is that we already have a fairly good public sector, which has good systems at the bottom. This enables us to deliver good services to our citizens today. This means that the need to be far ahead of blockchain in the short term is smaller, simply because short-term benefits could be less because a lot is already working well. But the upside is that the potential here is so huge. One has the public sector, one has the entrepreneurial environment, one has the academic environment, one has the business life we have in Norway, with very high trust between them, and with very low thresholds. It makes us want to be part of and be a driver of that development, so that we deliver services tomorrow that are much better than the ones we have today. Once again, the work to be done by the government to meet the objectives of the proposal is important, that it should be done both quickly and well, and that it is natural that both the political debate and, not least, the professional debate continue in our society, so that we exploit that potential in the best possible way. Making sure that technology doesn't become something that's arm's length away from a lot of the population, that people feel alienated from, but on the contrary, that there's something that contributes to more transparency, to more involvement, and that people understand how it works, I think everyone will experience that we, representing the public part of Norway, will deliver better services to the citizens.
